SILAGE: Memory-Efficient, Full-Gradient-Free Nonconvex Optimization for Nested Finite Sums Thanks: Center of Excellence for Generative AI, King Abdullah University of Science and Technology (KAUST), Thuwal, Saudi Arabia. Correspondence to: Igor Sokolov <igor.sokolov.1@kaust.edu.sa> .
Igor Sokolov, Laurent Condat, Peter Richtárik

Abstract
Empirical risk minimization on massive datasets naturally exhibits a nested double finite-sum structure, where N = n ​ m N=nm total samples are logically or physically partitioned into n n blocks of size m m (e.g., in pooled data silos, out-of-core learning, or deliberate stratification). While variance-reduced methods achieve optimal oracle complexities for nonconvex objectives, they suffer from severe scaling bottlenecks in this centralized regime. Recursive estimators, such as PAGE , require periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all n ​ m nm samples, which are computationally expensive. Conversely, single-loop methods, such as SILVER , avoid such refreshes but require an impractical 𝒪 ⁡ ( n ​ m ) {\cal O}\left(nm\right) memory footprint to store a control variate for every sample. In this paper, we propose SILAGE , a variance-reduced algorithm that addresses this trade-off. By actively exploiting the double-sum structure, SILAGE eliminates periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all n ​ m nm components—evaluating at most one local group gradient per iteration—while requiring only 𝒪 ⁡ ( n ) {\cal O}\left(n\right) memory. Furthermore, we provide a tight convergence analysis that avoids pessimistic worst-case Lipschitz constants. Instead, SILAGE ’s complexity natively adapts to the underlying data geometry via nested functional similarities: across-group ( δ 1 \delta_{1} ) and within-group ( δ 2 \delta_{2} ) heterogeneity. Our results improve existing state-of-the-art bounds in several practically relevant regimes.



1
Introduction

1.1
The centralized double-sum paradigm

Nonconvex optimization is ubiquitous across all areas of machine learning and artificial intelligence. In particular, empirical risk minimization (ERM) is the dominant paradigm in modern large-scale settings for supervised model training. While ERM is typically formulated as a flat finite sum of
N
components, modern centralized training pipelines increasingly exhibit a
nested double-sum structure

N=nm
, where data are logically or physically partitioned into
n
groups, blocks, or silos, each containing
m
samples
[
19
,
28
,
11
,
5
,
35
,
47
,
40
]
.

Thus, we consider nonconvex double finite-sum problems of the form

\min\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[f(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{f_{i}(x)}\right]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\quad\text{with}\quad f_{i}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)}\mathchar 59\relax

(1)

where
d
is the model dimension,
[n]\coloneqq\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n\}
, and
[m]\coloneqq\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}m\}
. The possibly nonconvex function
f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
typically represents the loss on the
j
-th sample in the
i
-th group, and
f_{i}
is the averaged objective function of that specific group.

Our analysis assumes that the aggregate objective
f
is lower bounded and
L
-smooth for some
L>0
. Crucially, however, we do
not
require every individual
f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
to be smooth, in contrast to many existing methods (see Section
2
for our detailed assumptions). While standard gradient descent solves (
1
) with a gradient evaluation complexity
1
1

1

Here and throughout, the notation
g(t)={\cal O}\left(h(t)\right)
means that there exist a constant
C>0
and a threshold
t_{0}
such that
|g(t)|\leq C\,h(t)
for all
t\geq t_{0}
; that is,
h
bounds
g
up to a universal multiplicative constant.
of
{\cal O}\left(nmL\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right)
, variance-reduced (VR) stochastic gradient methods are now standard tools for reducing this complexity
[
16
,
12
,
25
,
36
,
41
,
39
]
.

Exploiting this two-layer partition is vital because it naturally maps to several critical, single-machine settings where standard flat VR methods fail to scale
[
38
,
3
,
14
]
:

•

Data Silos and Pooled Data Lakes:
Imagine a centralized data center where data are legally or physically partitioned into
n
distinct “silos.” For example, a healthcare consortium aggregating patient records from
n
different hospitals, or an enterprise training on
n
different data modalities (text, images, logs) stored on a centralized cluster. While the computation is centralized—avoiding the communication latency and synchronization bottlenecks of federated learning—the data inherently retain their siloed structure. Each silo
i
contains
m
samples that share regional demographics or domain-specific features.

•

Out-of-Core and Memory-Constrained Learning:
In massive-scale training, such as optimizing foundation models on billions of tokens
[
32
,
4
]
, the dataset
N
vastly exceeds GPU VRAM or main memory. Data must be stored on slower NVMe/SSDs and loaded in chunks. Here,
n
represents the number of physical storage blocks (or shards), and
m
is the block size. Algorithms that require maintaining a state (control variate) for every single sample
j\in[N]
incur an
{\cal O}\left(N\right)
memory footprint, which is impractical in this regime. Conversely, storing just
n
control variates (one per block) reduces this memory overhead by a factor of
m
.

•

Deliberate Stratification and Clustering:
Even if the data are provided as a flat array, an optimizer can actively induce a double-sum structure to accelerate training. By clustering data via lightweight preprocessing—such as grouping by class labels or applying
k
-means to input features—the dataset is partitioned into
n
highly homogeneous clusters. This stratification creates a favorable variance geometry that our algorithm can natively exploit.

We emphasize that our setting is grouped (nested) finite-sum optimization in the centralized regime: federated and client-partitioned data serve as motivating special cases rather than the object of study, and our guarantees bound gradient (oracle) complexity and memory, not communication complexity.

1.2
Related work and contributions

All existing VR estimators exhibit scaling limitations at massive sizes, forcing a difficult trade-off between computation and memory.

The first category consists of methods that necessitate periodic global
full-gradient refreshes
over all
nm
samples.
This includes estimators such as
SVRG

[
16
,
20
]
,
SCSG

[
22
]
, and
SSRGD

[
24
]
, as well as recursive estimators such as
SARAH

[
30
]
,

SPIDER

[
12
]
,
SpiderBoost

[
46
]
, and
PAGE

[
25
,
44
]
(also independently proposed as Loopless-SARAH (
L2S
)

[
23
]
). Periodic global full-gradient refreshes are expensive and limit the scalability of these methods on very large datasets.

The second category comprises memory-based alternatives, such as classical
SAG
/
SAGA
-type estimators
[
37
,
9
,
2
,
10
]
and recent methods like
ZeroSARAH

[
26
]
and
SILVER

[
31
]
. These methods successfully avoid periodic global full-gradient refreshes. However, when applied to a flattened dataset, they suffer from an impractical
{\cal O}\left(nmd\right)
memory footprint
required to store a
d
-dimensional control variate for every individual sample.

Thus, in the centralized large-scale regime considered here, the primary bottlenecks are
gradient evaluation complexity
and
memory footprint
. The
n
-group structure lets us navigate this trade-off. We introduce
SILAGE
(
SIngle Loop Average Gradient Estimator
), a novel variance-reduced algorithm tailored for double finite-sum problems. Our contributions are organized along three structural axes— memory footprint, avoidance of periodic global full-gradient refreshes, and dependence of the convergence rate on the data geometry—together with a unification result that places
SILAGE
within the existing landscape:

•

Memory Efficiency via Nested Structure:
By maintaining
n
control-variate table entries (one per block), plus
{\cal O}(1)
auxiliary
d
-vectors—namely the running aggregate
g^{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}^{t}
that supplies the descent direction in Algorithms
1
and
2
—
SILAGE
requires only
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
memory
2
2

2

Here “
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
memory” counts, beyond the iterate itself, the
n+{\cal O}(1)
additional
d
-dimensional vectors just described. In
float64
precision this is approximately
8nd
bytes for large
n
, ignoring lower-order implementation overhead such as sampled indices, array metadata, and temporary workspace.
—a substantial reduction compared to the prohibitively large
{\cal O}\left(nm\right)
footprint of flattened methods such as
SILVER
.

•

No Periodic Global Full-Gradient Refresh:

SILAGE
never performs a global full-gradient pass over all
nm
components: each anchor reset evaluates only a single
local
group gradient
\nabla f_{i^{t}}(x^{t+1})
, at an
{\cal O}\left(m\right)
per-iteration cost. The method also permits arbitrary initial estimators
g_{i}^{0}
, so it requires neither the global full gradient
\nabla f(x^{0})
nor the local full gradients
\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})
at initialization; all initialization error enters the complexity only through the Lyapunov term
\Psi^{0}
(Corollaries
1
and
2
).

•

Exploitation of Nested Similarity:
We derive two-regime nonconvex convergence rates (for
m\geq n
and
n>m
) that explicitly leverage nested similarity constants:
\delta_{1}
(across-group) and
\delta_{2}
(within-group). This structured decomposition is fundamentally more expressive and informative than standard flattened similarity bounds (detailed in Section
2
).

•

Exact Limiting Reductions (unification):
Under matching parameter choices,
SILAGE
unifies existing approaches by exactly recovering
PAGE
(when
n=1
) and
SILVER
(when
m=1\mathchar 59\relax b=1
), hence the algorithm’s name.

At the level of building blocks,
SILAGE
inherits two standard ideas:
PAGE
-style recursive gradient tracking and the stored control variates of memory-based (
SAGA
/
SILVER
-style) estimators. Its novelty lies in how these are combined and analyzed for the nested problem: (i) an
estimator coupling
that drives all
n
group estimators through a single global coin and one uniformly sampled group, so that at most one group is fully refreshed per iteration; (ii) storing one control variate
per group
rather than per sample, giving the
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
footprint in place of the flattened
{\cal O}\left(nm\right)
; (iii) a
shared-drift
update that handles the
n>m
regime by correcting un-sampled groups without fresh per-group gradients; and (iv) a
two-level
nonconvex analysis in the nested similarity constants
\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2}
.

Terminology.

We use the following terms consistently throughout. A
periodic global full-gradient refresh
is the repeated evaluation, once every few iterations, of the full gradient
\nabla f(x)=\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)
over all
nm
components, as required by
PAGE
-style estimators; we reserve the word
refresh
for this recurring operation. It is distinct from a single
local group gradient

\nabla f_{i}
, which touches only the
m
components of one group at an
{\cal O}\left(m\right)
cost and is the only full-gradient computation
SILAGE
performs (at each anchor reset). We further distinguish two one-time choices for the initial estimators
g_{i}^{0}
: under
exact initialization
one sets
g_{i}^{0}=\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})
for all
i\in[n]
, at a single
nm
component-gradient cost, whereas under
arbitrary initialization
the
g_{i}^{0}
are chosen freely (e.g.,
g_{i}^{0}=0
) with no initial full-gradient pass. Accordingly, the
full-gradient-free
property recorded in Table
1
refers solely to the absence of periodic global full-gradient refreshes and is independent of the initialization choice.

2
Assumptions

We first recall standard assumptions from nonconvex optimization. The following two assumptions are classical and are used in essentially the same form in the analysis of gradient descent and modern variance-reduced methods for nonconvex finite sums
[
29
,
25
,
26
,
44
]
.

Assumption 1

(Lower boundedness)
.

There exists
f_{\mathrm{inf}}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f(x)\geq f_{\mathrm{inf}}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Assumption 2

(Smoothness of the average objective)
.

There exists
L\geq 0
such that, for all
x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|\leq L\left\|x-y\right\|.

A key point of our setting is that Assumption
2
is imposed strictly on the average objective
f
. Unless explicitly stated otherwise, we do not require every component
f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
to be individually smooth; they are merely assumed to be differentiable. Instead, we control the variance geometry of the double finite sum in (
1
) through two nested similarity conditions.

Assumption 3

(Across-group similarity)
.

There exists
\delta_{1}\geq 0
such that, for all
x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)+\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{1}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}.

(2)

Assumption 4

(Within-group similarity)
.

There exists
\delta_{2}\geq 0
such that, for all
x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)+\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}.

(3)

Assumption
3
measures how much the group gradients deviate from the global gradient, while Assumption
4
measures the average deviation of sample gradients from their respective group averages.

These are structural conditions of a different nature than component-wise smoothness: the latter is one sufficient condition for them, yet they can be much smaller than the worst-case component smoothness in favorable grouped and statistical regimes. For example, if all
f_{i}
are
L_{1}
-smooth, then (
2
) holds with
\delta_{1}\leq L_{1}
; similarly, a mean-squared smoothness bound on the individual functions
f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
implies (
3
). In many distributed and statistical regimes, similarity constants can be substantially smaller than pessimistic smoothness bounds
[
7
,
21
,
1
,
42
,
15
,
18
,
17
,
6
]
. Furthermore, in i.i.d. ERM settings, related similarity quantities decrease proportionately with the local sample size under standard statistical assumptions
[
45
,
48
]
. Importantly,
SILAGE
still assumes the average objective
f
is
L
-smooth (Assumption
2
), and only avoids dependence on component-wise or worst-case smoothness.

While similarity assumptions are a well-established tool in variance reduction
[
17
,
31
]
, the vast majority of existing baselines, including
SILVER

[
31
]
and
PAGE

[
44
]
, consider only a “flattened” similarity assumption of the form:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)-\nabla f(x)+\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}.

(4)

Note that (
4
) is linked to (
2
) and (
3
) via a simple algebraic equality:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla f(x)-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)+\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)+\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)+\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}.

Hence Assumptions
3
–
4
imply (
4
) with

\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}\leq\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{2}+\delta_{2}^{2}}.

Conversely, a flat bound controls both nested residuals, but the minimal constants on the two sides need not be related by a clean equality, since a supremum over
x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y
of a sum need not equal the sum of the individual suprema. The two-parameter representation by the pair
(\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2})
is therefore strictly more informative and provides a significantly finer algorithmic characterization. For instance, if there is no within-group variation, then
\delta_{2}=0
and
\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}=\delta_{1}
; conversely, if there is no across-group variation, then
\delta_{1}=0
and
\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}=\delta_{2}
. As we will demonstrate,
SILAGE
successfully decouples these dependencies depending on the operational regime.

In the optimization literature, component-level smoothness assumptions are typically formulated as follows
[
25
,
44
,
26
,
43
,
49
]
:

\displaystyle\textstyle\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq L_{i}\left\|x-y\right\|\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathchar 59\relax

(5)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\left\|x-y\right\|\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathchar 59\relax

(6)

for some constants
L_{i}\mathchar 59\relax L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}<\infty
. The worst-case component smoothness is commonly denoted by
L_{\max}:=\max_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
. Additionally, some analyses utilize an averaged smoothness assumption:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}\leq L_{+}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathchar 59\relax

(7)

for some
L_{+}<\infty
. These classical assumptions naturally imply a hierarchy of smoothness constants:

L^{2}\leq L_{+}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{2}\leq L_{\max}^{2}.

Relying on these inequalities, existing methods yield oracle complexities dominated by the larger constants in this hierarchy. For instance,
PAGE

[
25
]
and
ZeroSARAH

[
26
]
achieve theoretical oracle complexities of
{\cal O}\left(nm+L_{+}\sqrt{nm}\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right)
and
{\cal O}\left(nm+L_{\max}\sqrt{nm}\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right)
, respectively.

The Hessian-variance framework proposed by
Tyurin et al. 2023
tightens this dependence. Under uniform weights in the flattened regime, it yields a complexity of

{\cal O}\left(nm+\left(L+\sqrt{nm}\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}\right)\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right).

Furthermore, in the grouped regime under full client participation, it yields

{\cal O}\left(nm+\left(nL+\sqrt{nm}\bar{\delta}_{2}\right)\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
\bar{\delta}_{2}^{2}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}^{2}
, and
\delta_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}\geq 0
is a localized within-group constant such that, for all
x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)+\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}.

(8)

We emphasize that the total oracle complexity of
SILAGE
depends strictly on
L
,
\delta_{1}
, and
\delta_{2}
, entirely bypassing any dependence on the potentially huge worst-case constant
L_{\max}
. This structural advantage makes our theoretical rates significantly tighter in heterogeneous regimes compared to existing component-smoothness approaches.

The fundamental novelty of considering two layers of similarity (
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
) is not that their combination is always strictly smaller than the flattened similarity. Rather, Assumptions
3
and
4
rigorously decompose the flattened residual into distinct between-group and within-group components. As we demonstrate in our complexity analysis,
SILAGE
natively exploits this explicit separation: depending on the dataset dimensions, either
\delta_{2}
alone dictates the leading complexity (in the
m\geq n
regime), or both constants enter as multipliers of different dimensional terms (in the
n>m
regime). To the best of our knowledge, prior variance-reduction guarantees for double finite sums do not yield this two-level dependence on both
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
while maintaining an
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
memory footprint via a single estimator per group.

This dual-parameterization
(\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2})
provides a fundamentally more informative view of the geometry of (
1
) than a single flattened similarity constant. By decoupling distinct sources of heterogeneity—where
\delta_{1}
measures the variation of the group objectives
f_{i}
around the global objective
f
, and
\delta_{2}
measures the average variation of the component functions
f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
around their local group averages
f_{i}
—this framework naturally captures several practically relevant data regimes:

•

Globally and locally homogeneous data (
\delta_{1}
small,
\delta_{2}
small).

This regime arises when the groups are mutually similar and each group is internally coherent. Examples include repeated measurements collected under nearly identical experimental conditions, data acquired across standardized sites following the same protocol, or stable temporal windows of a slowly varying process. In such settings, both across-group and within-group deviations are mild.

•

Redundant blocks (
\delta_{1}
small,
\delta_{2}
relatively large).

This regime is induced by the random sharding of a large dataset: each group behaves as a representative subsample of the global population. Consequently, different groups are statistically close to one another (
\delta_{1}
is small), while the internal sample-level diversity inside each group remains substantial (
\delta_{2}
is large relative to
\delta_{1}
). This situation is common in distributed ERM when data are partitioned purely for computational scaling rather than semantic grouping.

•

Homogeneous silos (
\delta_{1}
large,
\delta_{2}
small).

Here, each group is internally coherent, but different groups correspond to genuinely distinct populations, domains, tasks, or modalities. Examples include clustered federated learning, client-specific personalization, or task-based partitions. Within-group variability is minimal, but the discrepancy between local objectives is pronounced.

•

Heterogeneous silos (
\delta_{1}
large,
\delta_{2}
large).

This regime captures challenging large-scale settings in which different groups correspond to distinct populations and each individual group is itself internally diverse. Examples include hierarchical federated learning architectures
[
27
]
spanning institutions with different demographics and broad case mixes, cross-market recommendation systems, or multi-domain corpora. In these cases, both levels of heterogeneity are substantial.

A single flattened similarity constant inherently obscures these qualitatively different geometries. By contrast, the
(\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2})
pair makes the multiscale heterogeneity explicit, allowing the resulting oracle complexity bounds to cleanly separate the structural impact of across-group and within-group variation.

Crucially,
SILAGE
is
agnostic
to these parameters during execution; it does not require prior knowledge of whether
\delta_{1}
or
\delta_{2}
dominates. It naturally adapts to whatever underlying data geometry is present, yielding theoretical complexities that strictly improve a posteriori based on the relative tightness of
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
.

3

Algorithm and Convergence Results

To obtain a single-loop estimator that entirely avoids periodic global full-gradient refreshes while exploiting the two-level structure of (
1
),
SILAGE
relies on a carefully constructed fusion of two variance-reduction philosophies: it applies a
SILVER
-like memory structure across the
n
groups, and a
PAGE
-like recursive tracking within the
m
components of each group. However, naively deploying
n
independent
PAGE
estimators (one for each group) would fail to yield a practical algorithm. In standard
PAGE
, an estimator is refreshed with the exact gradient with probability
1/m
. If we ran
n
independent coin flips per iteration, the number of groups triggering a full refresh simultaneously would be a binomial random variable. Consequently, the number of component gradients evaluated per iteration would be
nm
in the worst case, which is precisely what we want to avoid. To guarantee a strictly bounded maximum batch size,
SILAGE
carefully couples the two strategies. The group estimators are correlated in a specific way
that depends on the aspect ratio of the dataset:

•

The
m\geq n
regime (Algorithm
1
):
Our design target is that each of the
n
group estimators be fully refreshed with average probability
1/m
per iteration, matching the refresh rate of a
PAGE
estimator on a block of
m
components. To meet this target while never refreshing more than one group per iteration, we couple a
single global coin
with a uniform group selection: with probability
p
the coin lands heads, and conditioned on heads we select exactly
one
group
i^{t}
uniformly at random to undergo a full local refresh
\nabla f_{i^{t}}(x^{t+1})
. Under this scheme, the marginal probability that any fixed group is refreshed in a given iteration is
p\cdot\frac{1}{n}
; equating this with the target rate
1/m
yields
p=n/m
, which is a valid probability precisely because
m\geq n
. Thus Algorithm
1
evaluates at most one local group gradient
\nabla f_{i^{t}}
per iteration, and only on reset iterations (those for which the coin lands heads); consequently, the maximum batch size stays at
{\cal O}\left(m+n\right)={\cal O}\left(m\right)
. In this regime, the
PAGE
estimator is fully operational on each block independently; thus, the blocks do not interfere with each other, and across-group heterogeneity (
\delta_{1}
) does not affect the leading complexity.

•

The
n>m
regime (Algorithm
2
):
The previous strategy fails because
n/m>1
.
To maintain an
{\cal O}\left(m\right)
per-iteration cost, we cannot compute multiple (ideally
n/m
) group refreshes
\nabla f_{i}
, so we compute exactly one.
Moreover, updating the remaining
n-1
estimators with fresh local data would cost
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
, breaking our
{\cal O}\left(m\right)
budget. We circumvent this by selecting only a small subset of
b
groups (typically
b=m
) to receive fresh gradient evaluations. There is no gradient evaluation for the remaining un-sampled groups; instead, their estimators are updated using a
shared drift
computed exclusively from the
b
active groups. We pay a theoretical price for this cost-saving design: because the un-sampled groups rely on updates generated by other groups, the across-group heterogeneity (
\delta_{1}
) enters the convergence complexity.

At the exact boundary where
n=m
, both formulations naturally coincide under the right parameter choices. In the following subsections, we state the convergence guarantees for both regimes. Both versions store exactly one estimator
g_{i}^{t}
for each group
f_{i}
and use their average
g^{t}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}^{t}
as the descent direction.

3.1
Convergence in the
m\geq n
regime

We first state our main convergence result covering the
m\geq n
regime.

Theorem 1

(General convergence,
m\geq n
)
.

Let
1
(lower-boundednes),
2
(
L
-smoothness), and
4
(sithin-group
\delta_{2}
-similarity) hold, with
m\geq n
. In
SILAGE
(Algorithm
1
),
assume the stepsize satisfies

0<\gamma\leq\left(L+\delta_{2}\sqrt{\frac{n-p}{np}}\right)^{-1}.

For any
T\geq 0
,

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\tilde{x}^{T})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\Psi^{0}}{\gamma(T+1)}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

(9)

where

\tilde{x}^{T}
is chosen uniformly at random from the iterates
\{x^{0}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}x^{T}\}
, and

\Psi^{0}:=f(x^{0})-f_{\mathrm{inf}}+\frac{\gamma n}{4p}\left\|g^{0}-\nabla f(x^{0})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\gamma}{4np}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{0}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})\right\|^{2}.

The above result directly implies the following iteration complexity bound.

Corollary 1

(Complexity,
m\geq n
)
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
1
, choose the largest admissible stepsize, i.e.,

\gamma=\left(L+\delta_{2}\sqrt{\frac{n-p}{np}}\right)^{-1}.

Then the iteration complexity of
SILAGE
to reach an
\epsilon
-approximate stationary point is

{\cal O}\left(\left(L+\frac{\delta_{2}}{\sqrt{p}}\right)\frac{\Psi^{0}}{\epsilon}\right)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

(10)

and there are
{\cal O}\left(pm+n\right)
component-gradient evaluations per iteration.
In particular, by choosing
p=\frac{n}{m}
, the gradient complexity is

{\cal O}\left(\left(nL+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\right)\frac{\Psi^{0}}{\epsilon}\right).

Furthermore, with
exact initialization (
g_{i}^{0}=\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})\ \forall i\in[n]
), we have
\Psi^{0}=\Delta_{0}:=f(x^{0})-f_{\mathrm{inf}}
, and the gradient complexity is

{\cal O}\left(nm+\left(nL+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\right)\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right).

Crucially, because Algorithm
1
utilizes the single-coin strategy and updates estimators strictly with their own local data, the estimator error relies exclusively on the within-group variability. Consequently, only
\delta_{2}
appears in the bound.

3.2
Convergence in the
n>m
regime

For the
n>m
regime, in
SILAGE
(Algorithm
2
), we refresh exactly one group
i^{t}
and select an active subset
\Omega^{t}
of size
b_{\mathrm{grp}}-1
for recursive updates. For the remaining un-sampled groups, the algorithm applies the
shared
recursive drift
d^{t}
computed from the active subset. Because this shared correction forces estimators to track directions generated by different groups, its variance relies on both the across-group (
\delta_{1}
) and within-group (
\delta_{2}
) heterogeneities.

Theorem 2

(General Convergence,
n>m
)
.

Consider the
n>m
regime, and let
1
(lower-boundednes),
2
(
L
-smoothness),
3
(across-group
\delta_{1}
similarity) and
4
(within-group
\delta_{2}
-similarity) hold. In
SILAGE
(Algorithm
2
),
assume the stepsize satisfies

0<\gamma\leq\left(L+\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{2}\frac{(n-b_{\mathrm{grp}})(5n+4)}{n-1}+\delta_{2}^{2}\frac{n^{2}+2n-7b_{\mathrm{grp}}+4b_{\mathrm{grp}}^{2}}{2b_{\mathrm{grp}}n}}\right)^{-1}.

For any
T\geq 0
,

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\tilde{x}^{T})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\Psi^{0}}{\gamma(T+1)}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

(11)

where

\tilde{x}^{T}
is chosen uniformly at random from the iterates,
and

\Psi^{0}:=f(x^{0})-f_{\mathrm{inf}}+\frac{\gamma n}{4}\left\|g^{0}-\nabla f(x^{0})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\gamma}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{0}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})\right\|^{2}.

The above result directly implies the following iteration complexity bound.

Corollary 2

(Complexity,
n>m
)
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
2
, choose the largest admissible stepsize, i.e.,

\gamma=\left(L+\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{2}\frac{(n-b_{\mathrm{grp}})(5n+4)}{n-1}+\delta_{2}^{2}\frac{n^{2}+2n-7b_{\mathrm{grp}}+4b_{\mathrm{grp}}^{2}}{2b_{\mathrm{grp}}n}}\right)^{-1}.

Then the iteration complexity
3
3

3

Here and throughout, we write
\Psi_{b_{\mathrm{grp}}}^{0}
, with the explicit subscript, whenever we wish to emphasize the dependence of the initial Lyapunov quantity on the batch-size parameter
b_{\mathrm{grp}}
; the plain symbol
\Psi^{0}
denotes the same quantity with this dependence left implicit.
of
SILAGE
to reach an
\epsilon
-approximate stationary point is

{\cal O}\left(\left(L+\sqrt{\delta_{1}^{2}\frac{(n-b_{\mathrm{grp}})(5n+4)}{n-1}+\delta_{2}^{2}\frac{n^{2}+2n-7b_{\mathrm{grp}}+4b_{\mathrm{grp}}^{2}}{2b_{\mathrm{grp}}n}}\right)\frac{\Psi_{b_{\mathrm{grp}}}^{0}}{\epsilon}\right)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

(12)

and there are
{\cal O}\left(m+b_{\mathrm{grp}}\right)
component-gradient evaluations per iteration.

In particular, by choosing
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=m
, the gradient complexity is

{\cal O}\left(\left(mL+m\sqrt{n-m}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\right)\frac{\Psi_{m}^{0}}{\epsilon}\right).

Furthermore, with
exact initialization (
g_{i}^{0}=\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})\ \forall i\in[n]
), we have
\Psi_{m}^{0}=\Delta_{0}:=f(x^{0})-f_{\mathrm{inf}}
, and the gradient complexity is

{\cal O}\left(nm+\left(mL+m\sqrt{n-m}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\right)\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right).

The drift update in Algorithm
2
is written to touch all
n
estimators, which is convenient for the analysis but would suggest an
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
per-iteration cost. Appendix
D.1
removes this overhead: storing each estimator in the shifted form
g_{i}^{t}=h_{i}^{t}+q^{t}
with a single shared accumulator
q^{t}
, the equivalent Algorithm
3
applies the shared drift via one update of
q^{t}
and refreshes only the
\tilde{\Omega}^{t}
active entries; Lemma
9
proves the two forms generate identical iterates, so the implementation realizes the
{\cal O}\left(m+b_{\mathrm{grp}}\right)
per-iteration cost.

Note that when
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=m
, the coefficient multiplying
\delta_{1}
becomes
m\sqrt{n-m}
. This term vanishes at the exact boundary
n=m
, yielding algebraic equivalence with the bounds from the
m\geq n
regime. At the opposite extreme, when
m=1
and
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=1
, the subset
\Omega^{t}
is empty, and

SILAGE
reverts to
SILVER
, with our complexity matching the one in
Oko et al. 2024
.

3.3
Comparison with closely related baselines

In this section, we compare the theoretical guarantees of
SILAGE
against closely related variance-reduced baselines under exact initialization. This is the fairest main-text comparison to methods that begin from the true full gradient, while keeping the core discussion focused. The complementary comparison under arbitrary initialization is deferred to Appendix
A.2
.

3.3.1
Exact initialization (full-gradient)

We now compare the algorithms under exact initialization, where
g_{i}^{0}=\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})
for all
i\in[n]
. In this setting,
g^{0}=\nabla f(x^{0})
and the initial Lyapunov quantity perfectly collapses to
\Psi^{0}=\Delta_{0}:=f(x^{0})-f_{\mathrm{inf}}
, at the cost of
nm
component-gradient evaluations.

We emphasize that
SILAGE
does not require exact initialization to enjoy its theoretical convergence guarantees: arbitrary initial estimators
g_{i}^{0}
are admissible, and the initialization error enters the complexity only through the Lyapunov quantity
\Psi^{0}
. The exact initialization adopted here is used solely for a like-for-like comparison with baselines that themselves rely on it.

To avoid overloading batch-size notation in the comparison, we write
b_{\mathrm{fp}}
for the flattened
PAGE
minibatch size,
b_{\mathrm{sv}}
for the
SILVER
minibatch size,
b_{\mathrm{zs}}
for the
ZeroSARAH
minibatch size,
(s_{\mathrm{dp}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}b_{\mathrm{dp}})
for the outer/client and local batch sizes of
D-PAGE
,
(s_{\mathrm{dzs}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}b_{\mathrm{dzs}})
for the client and local batch sizes of
D-ZeroSARAH
, and
b_{\mathrm{grp}}
for the group-batch parameter of
SILAGE
in the
n>m
regime; this is the parameter that appears in Algorithm
2
, Theorem
2
, and Corollary
2
.

The results of this comparison are illustrated in Table
3
, which reports the specialization of each oracle complexity under the canonical batch-size choice for the corresponding method. The canonical choices themselves, together with the full tunable form of each bound, are deferred to Appendix
A.1
(Table
5
).

. Every bound counts component-gradient evaluations
\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
and includes the one-time initialization cost
nm
. The
SILAGE
bounds are taken from its convergence corollaries, and the baseline bounds from the discussion in Section

.

Specializations under the canonical batch-size choices for each method. The choices themselves, as well as the full tunable form of each bound, are given in Appendix
A.1
, Table
5
.

(1)

All rows include the exact-initialization cost
nm
. For
SILAGE
, this corresponds to setting
g_{i}^{0}=\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})
for all
i\in[n]
, so that
\Psi^{0}=\Delta_{0}:=f(x^{0})-f_{\mathrm{inf}}
.

(2)

Here
\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
is the flattened similarity constant over all
nm
components, while
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
are the across-group and within-group similarity constants used by
SILAGE
. The quantity
\bar{\delta}_{2}
denotes the within-group similarity appearing in the double-sampling
D-PAGE
bound.

(3)

The constants
L_{+}
and
L_{\max}
are baseline-specific worst-case smoothness quantities. In contrast,
SILAGE
’s bounds are stated in terms of the smoothness
L
of the averaged objective together with the nested similarity constants
\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2}
.

Under Corollary
1
and
2
,
SILAGE
achieves an oracle complexity of

{\cal O}\left(nm+\left(nL+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\right)\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right)

for
m\geq n
, and

{\cal O}\left(nm+\left(mL+m\sqrt{n-m}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\right)\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right)

for
n>m
with
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=m
.

Comparison with
ZeroSARAH
variants.

With exact initialization, both
ZeroSARAH

[
26
]
and
D-ZeroSARAH

[
26
]
achieve

{\cal O}\left(nm+\sqrt{nm}\,L_{\max}\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right)

iteration complexity. Mirroring the arbitrary initialization case,
SILAGE
systematically improves upon this by replacing
L_{\max}
with the structured terms
L
,
\delta_{1}
, and
\delta_{2}
. In the
m\geq n
regime,
SILAGE
is superior whenever the within-group variability
\delta_{2}
and global smoothness
L
are small relative to the worst-case component smoothness
L_{\max}
.

Comparison with
SILVER
.

Run on the flattened problem,
SILVER

[
31
]
with exact initialization has a tuned complexity of

{\cal O}\left(nm+\left(L_{+}\vee\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}\right)\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right).

Crucially, this flattened implementation stores one estimator per sample, demanding an impractical
{\cal O}\left(nm\right)
memory.
SILAGE
reduces this to
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
memory while simultaneously improving the gradient complexity whenever the nested constants are substantially smaller than the flattened quantities (e.g.,
\sqrt{n/m}L+\delta_{2}\ll\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
when
m\geq n
). Consequently,
SILAGE
can be viewed as the nested analogue of
SILVER
: it recovers
SILVER
when
m=1
, but for genuine double finite sums, it structurally separates the nested geometry (
\delta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\delta_{2}
) instead of collapsing it into
\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
.

Comparison with
PAGE
variants.

PAGE
-type baselines warrant a separate analysis because, unlike single-loop methods, they structurally require periodic global full-gradient refreshes
even after
exact initialization. Let

\bar{\delta}_{2}^{2}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}^{2}

denote the averaged within-group similarity. Because a supremum of an average is at most the average of the suprema, these constants always satisfy
\delta_{2}\leq\bar{\delta}_{2}
, with a gap that can be large when the worst-case within-group geometry occurs at different points across groups.

For flattened
PAGE
(with Nice minibatch size
b_{\mathrm{fp}}
and refresh probability
p(b_{\mathrm{fp}})=b_{\mathrm{fp}}/(nm+b_{\mathrm{fp}})
), the complexity is

{\cal O}\left(nm+\frac{nm\,b_{\mathrm{fp}}}{nm+b_{\mathrm{fp}}}\left(L+\frac{\sqrt{nm(nm-b_{\mathrm{fp}})}}{b_{\mathrm{fp}}\sqrt{nm-1}}\,\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}\right)\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right).

Taking
b_{\mathrm{fp}}=n
(when
m\geq n
) and
b_{\mathrm{fp}}=m
(when
n>m
) yields leading factors of
nL+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
and
mL+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
, respectively. Like flattened
SILVER
, this inherently merges nested variability into a single constant.

The structured double-Nice
D-PAGE
baseline is considerably stronger. With outer/client batch size
s_{\mathrm{dp}}
, local batch size
b_{\mathrm{dp}}
, and refresh probability
p=s_{\mathrm{dp}}b_{\mathrm{dp}}/(nm+s_{\mathrm{dp}}b_{\mathrm{dp}})
, its theoretical cost is

{\cal O}\left(nm+\frac{nm\,s_{\mathrm{dp}}b_{\mathrm{dp}}}{nm+s_{\mathrm{dp}}b_{\mathrm{dp}}}\left(L+\sqrt{\frac{nm(m-b_{\mathrm{dp}})}{s_{\mathrm{dp}}^{2}b_{\mathrm{dp}}^{2}(m-1)}\,\bar{\delta}_{2}^{2}+\frac{nm(n-s_{\mathrm{dp}})}{s_{\mathrm{dp}}^{2}b_{\mathrm{dp}}(n-1)}\,\delta_{1}^{2}}\right)\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right).

•

Regime
m\geq n
:
The useful full-outer-sampling choice
s_{\mathrm{dp}}=n
and
b_{\mathrm{dp}}=1
gives a leading factor of
nL+\sqrt{nm}\,\bar{\delta}_{2}
. This matches the leading
SILAGE
dependence up to the distinction between
\delta_{2}
and
\bar{\delta}_{2}
. However,
D-PAGE
pays for this with periodic global full-gradient refreshes. Furthermore, since
\delta_{2}\leq\bar{\delta}_{2}
(above), with
\bar{\delta}_{2}
averaging client-wise worst-case constants and
\delta_{2}
a global within-group average,
SILAGE
is weakly preferable in this regime, and the gap can be substantial in heterogeneous settings.

•

Regime
n>m
:
The choice
s_{\mathrm{dp}}=m
and
b_{\mathrm{dp}}=1
gives a leading factor of
mL+\sqrt{m(n-m)}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{nm}\,\bar{\delta}_{2}
, against
mL+m\sqrt{n-m}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}
for the
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=m
specialization of
SILAGE
.
D-PAGE
is a strong full-refresh baseline here, and the two are
not
ordered coefficient-wise:
D-PAGE
has the smaller across-group coefficient (
\sqrt{m(n-m)}
versus
SILAGE
’s
m\sqrt{n-m}
, a factor of
\sqrt{m}
), whereas
SILAGE
has the smaller within-group constant, since
\delta_{2}\leq\bar{\delta}_{2}
. Thus
SILAGE
improves on
D-PAGE
only when its within-group advantage outweighs this across-group penalty, i.e. whenever
m\sqrt{n-m}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\ll\sqrt{m(n-m)}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{nm}\,\bar{\delta}_{2}
, and it does so without the periodic global full-gradient refreshes
D-PAGE
requires.

Ultimately, the cleanest distinction between
SILAGE
and
PAGE
-type methods is algorithmic rather than a uniform oracle-complexity improvement.
D-PAGE
can attain competitive leading factors and keeps a flat
{\cal O}\left(1\right)
memory footprint, but, like every
PAGE
-type estimator, it requires periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all
nm
components.
SILAGE
is instead single-loop: it removes these refreshes entirely, at the cost of
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
group-level memory (one estimator per group), while still exploiting the nested similarity structure. Its advantage over
D-PAGE
is therefore the elimination of periodic global refreshes—most valuable when such refreshes dominate cost—rather than a claim of improving every oracle-complexity term.

The constants
L
,
\delta_{1}
, and
\delta_{2}
are objects of the
analysis
: they determine the largest provably admissible stepsize and the resulting convergence rate, but they do not appear in the algorithm itself.
SILAGE
samples groups, flips the global coin, and performs anchor resets and recursive updates without ever referencing them, so a single run is identical regardless of their values. Calibrating a stepsize to problem constants is standard for variance-reduced methods—
L
for gradient descent,
L_{\max}
for
ZeroSARAH
—and the distinguishing feature of
SILAGE
is not independence from such constants but dependence on the
structured
quantities
L\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2}
rather than the pessimistic
L_{\max}
or the flattened
\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
; this is precisely what yields the larger admissible stepsize and the improved rates established above. In practice, the constants may be estimated whenever curvature access is available—as for the logistic-regression objectives in our experiments, where Hessian–vector products make
L\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2}
cheap to probe (Appendix
E
)—replaced by any valid upper bound, or sidestepped by a standard stepsize search, all without altering the algorithm. Our experiments adopt the first route: probe-set estimates are used in the theoretically admissible stepsize while batch sizes are tuned per method, so that no exact prior knowledge of these constants is assumed.

Figure 1
:
Optimization trajectories on the controlled nonconvex-regularized logistic regression task,
m\geq n
regime (
n=50
,
m=250
). Each panel fixes one of the four similarity regimes; the corresponding measured constants
(\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}L\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}L_{\max})
are reported in Table
4
.

4
Experiments

In this section, we empirically validate whether the theoretically predicted dependence on nested similarity parameters (
\delta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\delta_{2}
) translates to practical optimization performance. We consider a binary logistic regression task of the form (
1
) with a smooth, nonconvex regularizer applied identically across all components. In particular, for feature-label pairs
(a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\}
, the individual sample loss is

f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x):=\log(1+\exp(-y_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}x))+\lambda\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\frac{x_{\ell}^{2}}{(1+x_{\ell}^{2})}.

We set
\lambda=200
across all runs. This heavy regularization ensures the nonconvex penalty dominates the global smoothness scale.

Figure 2
:
Optimization trajectories on the controlled nonconvex-regularized logistic regression task,
n>m
regime (
n=250
,
m=50
). Each panel fixes one of the four similarity regimes; the corresponding measured constants
(\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}L\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}L_{\max})
are reported in Table
4
.

We construct eight synthetic grouped datasets to test two distinct shape regimes:
m\geq n
with
(n\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}m)=(50\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}250)
, and
n>m
with
(n\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}m)=(250\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}50)
. Within each regime, we synthesize four controlled datasets to independently realize small or large across-group similarity (
\delta_{1}
) alongside small or large within-group similarity (
\delta_{2}
). The source code, together with instructions for reproducing Figures
1
and
2
, is available at
github.com/IgorSokoloff/silage-experiments
. Full details regarding data generation, Hessian-vector product estimation of
L\mathchar 59\relax\delta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\delta_{2}
, and implementation specifics are deferred to Appendix
E
. Table
4
summarizes the empirically measured constants for all eight datasets, confirming that our construction realizes the intended relative magnitudes of
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
; it also gives the precise quantitative meaning of the qualitative “small/large” labels used in the panels of Figures
1
and
2
.

Table 4
:

Empirical geometric diagnostics measured on the synthetic datasets. The values confirm that our construction successfully controls the relative magnitudes of across-group similarity (
\delta_{1}
) and within-group similarity (
\delta_{2}
), while maintaining a consistently large global smoothness scale (
L\approx 400
).

We compare
SILAGE
against three baselines that successfully avoid periodic global full-gradient refreshes: flattened
ZeroSARAH

[
26
]
, flattened
SILVER

[
31
]
, and the nested distributed variant
D-ZeroSARAH

[
26
]
. We intentionally omit
PAGE
and
D-PAGE
, whose estimators fundamentally rely on periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all
nm
components
[
25
,
44
]
; the empirical comparison thus isolates the no-refresh design class, while the oracle-cost of those refreshes is quantified analytically in Section
3.3.1
(Table
3
). We deploy Algorithm
1
for the
m\geq n
regime and Algorithm
2
for the
n>m
regime. All methods use the largest theoretically admissible constant stepsize from their respective analyses, evaluated with empirical estimates of the relevant smoothness and similarity constants; batch-size parameters are tuned per method, with the full protocol (probe-set estimation, tuning grids, and fallback rule) described in Appendix
E.6
. For
SILAGE
in the
n>m
regime, the headline specialization
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=m
gives a clean closed-form rate but is not necessarily oracle-optimal: Theorem
2
holds for every
b_{\mathrm{grp}}\in[n]
, the oracle-optimal choice is problem-dependent (Appendix
D.4
), and we accordingly tune
b_{\mathrm{grp}}
over a grid, so the selected value can be smaller than
m
. We report the exact squared gradient norm,
\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}
, recomputed every two iterations, until reaching
\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\leq 10^{-12}
or exhausting the epoch budget.

Across these controlled synthetic runs, Figures
1
and
2
show
SILAGE
to be the fastest, or among the fastest, of the tested methods, with the empirical trajectories closely tracking our theoretical predictions. In the
m\geq n
regime (Figure
1
), our theory indicates the leading similarity term depends entirely on
\delta_{2}
, ignoring
\delta_{1}
. The plots are consistent with this: in both small-
\delta_{2}
panels,
SILAGE
converges rapidly even when the across-group heterogeneity
\delta_{1}
is large. While a large
\delta_{2}
naturally increases the problem difficulty,
SILAGE
still outperforms both
ZeroSARAH
variants and matches or exceeds
SILVER
in these runs. In the
n>m
regime (Figure
2
), where both
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
factor into the bound,
SILAGE
outperforms the tested baselines across all four similarity profiles shown. Taken together, these controlled experiments support our theoretical predictions, indicating a practical benefit from explicitly separating and exploiting nested similarity rather than relying on flattened structures or pessimistic worst-case component smoothness.

We emphasize that these experiments are a controlled synthetic validation of the predicted similarity-dependence rather than a systems study: the
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
memory and scalability advantages are established by construction and summarized in Table
1
, not measured here, and a real grouped-data or wall-clock evaluation is left to future work.

5
Additional Results

Several complementary results are deferred to the appendix. A full description of the experimental setup—covering data-generation protocols, the Hessian-vector-product estimation of
L
,
\delta_{1}
, and
\delta_{2}
, hyperparameter tuning, and implementation details—is provided in Appendix
E
. The Polyak–Łojasiewicz convergence analyses of
SILAGE
appear in Appendix
C.2
for the
m\geq n
regime and in Appendix
D.3
for the
n>m
regime. Finally, the corresponding complexity comparison against the same baselines under arbitrary (zero-full-gradient) initialization is given in Appendix
A.2
.

All rows include the exact-initialization cost
nm
. For
SILAGE
, this corresponds to setting
g_{i}^{0}=\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})
for all
i\in[n]
, so that
\Psi^{0}=\Delta_{0}:=f(x^{0})-f_{\mathrm{inf}}
.

(2)

Here
\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
is the flattened similarity constant over all
nm
components, while
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
are the across-group and within-group similarity constants used by
SILAGE
. The quantity
\bar{\delta}_{2}
denotes the within-group similarity appearing in the double-sampling
D-PAGE
bound.

(3)

The tunable parameters are: the minibatch size
b_{\mathrm{sv}}
in
SILVER
; the flattened
PAGE
minibatch size
b_{\mathrm{fp}}
; the outer/client batch size
s_{\mathrm{dp}}
and local batch size
b_{\mathrm{dp}}
in
D-PAGE
; the minibatch size
b_{\mathrm{zs}}
and weight
\lambda
in
ZeroSARAH
; the client batch size
s_{\mathrm{dzs}}
and local batch size
b_{\mathrm{dzs}}
in
D-ZeroSARAH
; and the group batch size
b_{\mathrm{grp}}
in
SILAGE
(
n>m
).

(4)

The constants
L_{+}
and
L_{\max}
are baseline-specific worst-case smoothness quantities. In contrast,
SILAGE
’s bounds are stated in terms of the smoothness
L
of the averaged objective together with the nested similarity constants
\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2}
.

A.2
Arbitrary initialization (zero full-gradient)

In this setting, we assume that the estimators
g_{i}
are initialized in an arbitrary manner (e.g.,
g_{i}^{0}=0
), bypassing the need to compute any full gradients at the start of the algorithm. We compare
SILAGE
against methods that similarly admit arbitrary initialization.

As in the main-text comparison,
b_{\mathrm{grp}}
denotes the group-batch parameter of
SILAGE
in the
n>m
regime, as defined in Algorithm
2
and used in Theorem
2
and Corollary
2
.

For
SILAGE
, Corollary
1
establishes an oracle complexity of
{\cal O}\left(\left(nL+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\right)\frac{\Psi^{0}}{\epsilon}\right)
in the
m\geq n
regime. In the
n>m
regime, the general bound yields
{\cal O}\left(\left(m+b_{\mathrm{grp}}\right)\left(L+\delta_{1}\sqrt{n-b_{\mathrm{grp}}}+\delta_{2}\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{b_{\mathrm{grp}}}}\right)\frac{\Psi_{b_{\mathrm{grp}}}^{0}}{\epsilon}\right)
. Specifically, the choice
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=m
gives
{\cal O}\left(\left(mL+m\sqrt{n-m}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\right)\frac{\Psi_{m}^{0}}{\epsilon}\right)
, while the endpoint
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=n
removes the across-group term entirely, yielding
{\cal O}\left(\left(nL+n\delta_{2}\right)\frac{\Psi_{n}^{0}}{\epsilon}\right)
.

Comparison with
ZeroSARAH
variants.

Flattened
ZeroSARAH
and
D-ZeroSARAH
achieve complexities of
{\cal O}\left(\sqrt{nm}\left(L_{\max}\Delta_{0}+G_{0}\right)/\epsilon\right)
and
{\cal O}\left(\sqrt{nm}\left(L_{\max}\Delta_{0}+G^{\prime}_{0}\right)/\epsilon\right)
, respectively. Here,
L_{\max}:=\max_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
is the worst-case component smoothness,
G_{0}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})\right\|^{2}
is the initial estimator error for
ZeroSARAH
, and
G_{0}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{0})\right\|^{2}
is the corresponding error for
D-ZeroSARAH
.

Thus, up to the differing initial Lyapunov quantities,
SILAGE
completely replaces the pessimistic worst-case component smoothness
L_{\max}
with the structured global parameters
L\mathchar 59\relax\delta_{1}
, and
\delta_{2}
.

•

For
m\geq n
,
SILAGE
is favorable whenever
nL+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\ll\sqrt{nm}\,L_{\max}
.

•

For
n>m
, the choice
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=m
is favorable whenever
mL+m\sqrt{n-m}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\ll\sqrt{nm}\,L_{\max}
. If
\delta_{1}
is large but the global
L
is much smaller than
L_{\max}
, the endpoint choice
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=n
can be preferable as it strictly removes the
\delta_{1}
dependence.

Comparison with
SILVER
.

The oracle complexity of flattened
SILVER
is
{\cal O}\left(\left(\Delta_{0}\left(\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}\sqrt{nm}\vee Lb_{\mathrm{sv}}\right)+\frac{nm}{b_{\mathrm{sv}}}\sigma_{\mathrm{flat}}^{2}\right)/\epsilon\right)
, where
b_{\mathrm{sv}}\in[nm]
is the flattened minibatch size and
\sigma_{\mathrm{flat}}^{2}:=\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{r=1}^{nm}\left\|\nabla\tilde{f}_{r}(x^{0})-\nabla f(x^{0})\right\|^{2}
measures the initial variance.

Assuming a low-initial-variance regime where
\Psi^{0}
and
\Delta_{0}
are comparable,
SILAGE
improves upon optimized, zero-initialized flattened
SILVER
under the following conditions:

•

Regime
m\geq n
:

SILAGE
is strictly better when
nL+\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{2}\ll\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
, or equivalently
\sqrt{n/m}L+\delta_{2}\ll\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
. This occurs when flattening is inherently costly—for instance, when the flat heterogeneity
\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
is primarily driven by across-group variation (
\delta_{1}
), which
SILAGE
entirely avoids paying for in this regime. If instead
\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}\approx\delta_{2}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{flat}}^{2}
is small, the theoretical rate matches
SILVER
, but
SILAGE
retains its massive
{\cal O}\left(n\right)
rather than
{\cal O}\left(nm\right)
memory advantage.

•

Regime
n>m
:
Comparing the leading factors for a chosen group batch size
b_{\mathrm{grp}}\in[m\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n]
,
SILAGE
is favorable when
\left(m+b_{\mathrm{grp}}\right)\left(L+\sqrt{n-b_{\mathrm{grp}}}\,\delta_{1}+\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{b_{\mathrm{grp}}}}\,\delta_{2}\right)\ll\sqrt{nm}\,\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
. For our targeted choice
b_{\mathrm{grp}}=m
, this simplifies to
\sqrt{m/n}L+\sqrt{m(n-m)/n}\,\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}\ll\delta_{\mathrm{flat}}
. Thus, the small-batch choice is highly effective when the scaled across-group term is small. If
\delta_{1}
is large, increasing
b_{\mathrm{grp}}
to
n
eliminates the across-group term entirely, proving preferable whenever
m\sqrt{n-m}\,\delta_{1}\gtrsim(n-m)L+(n-\sqrt{nm})\delta_{2}
(ignoring initial
\Psi^{0}
differences).

Appendix B
Auxiliary results

This section collects several auxiliary results that we repeatedly use in the proofs.
Most of them are elementary identities for smooth functions, conditional second moments, and uniform sampling without replacement; we state them explicitly to keep the main proof steps focused on the estimator recursions.

Lemma 1

(Inexact-gradient descent;
[
25
]
)
.

Let
f
be
L
-smooth, let
\gamma>0
, and let
x^{+}=x-\gamma g
for some
x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then

\displaystyle f(x^{+})\leq f(x)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\left\|g-\nabla f(x)\right\|^{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x)\right\|^{2}+\left(\frac{L}{2}-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\right)\left\|x^{+}-x\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

By
L
-smoothness,

\displaystyle f(x^{+})\leq f(x)+\left\langle\nabla f(x)\mathchar 59\relax x^{+}-x\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\left\|x^{+}-x\right\|^{2}.

Since
x^{+}-x=-\gamma g
, the identity

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla f(x)\mathchar 59\relax x^{+}-x\right\rangle=\frac{\gamma}{2}\left\|g-\nabla f(x)\right\|^{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x)\right\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left\|x^{+}-x\right\|^{2}

gives the claim.
∎

Lemma 2

(Conditional second-moment identities)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a sigma-algebra. For any square-integrable random vector
Z
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|Z\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right]=\left\|\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.Z\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right]\right\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|Z-\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.Z\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right]\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right].

(13)

Moreover, let
X
be
\mathcal{G}
-measurable and let
Y_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}Y_{K}
be conditionally independent given
\mathcal{G}
, with finite second moments and
\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.Y_{k}\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right]=0
for all
k
. Then, for any deterministic scalars
a_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}a_{K}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|X+\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}Y_{k}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right]=\left\|X\right\|^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|Y_{k}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right].

(14)

Proof.

The first identity follows by expanding

Z=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.Z\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right]+\left(Z-\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.Z\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right]\right)
and using that the second term has conditional mean zero.
For the second identity, expand the squared norm. The cross term with
X
vanishes because each
Y_{k}
has zero conditional mean, and the cross terms

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\langle Y_{k}\mathchar 59\relax Y_{\ell}\right\rangle\,\right|\mathcal{G}\right]
vanish for
k\neq\ell
by conditional independence and zero conditional means.
∎

Lemma 3

(Finite-population variance; Proposition 1 in
[
8
]
)
.

Let
N\geq 1
,
B\in[N]
, and vectors
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
. Let
v:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}
and let
\Omega
be a subset of
[N]
of size
B
chosen uniformly at random.
Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i\in\Omega}v_{i}-v\right\|^{2}\right]=\frac{N-B}{BN(N-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\|v_{i}-v\right\|^{2}

(15)

if
N\geq 2
, or equals zero if
N=B=1
.

Lemma 4

(Uniformity of anchored subset sampling)
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
B\in[N]
. Draw
i\sim\mathcal{U}([N])
and, conditionally on
i
, draw
\Omega
uniformly among all subsets of
[N]\setminus\{i\}
of size
B-1
. Set
\tilde{\Omega}:=\Omega\cup\{i\}
. Then
\tilde{\Omega}
is uniformly distributed over all subsets of
[N]
of size
B
.
Moreover, for any
k\in[N]
with
B<N
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Prob}(k\notin\tilde{\Omega})=\frac{N-B}{N}\mathchar 59\relax

and, conditionally on
\{k\notin\tilde{\Omega}\}
, the set
\tilde{\Omega}
is uniformly distributed over all subsets of
[N]\setminus\{k\}
of size
B
.

Proof.

Fix
S\subset[N]
with
|S|=B
. The event
\{\tilde{\Omega}=S\}
occurs exactly when
i=s
for some
s\in S
and
\Omega=S\setminus\{s\}
. Hence

\displaystyle\mathbf{Prob}(\tilde{\Omega}=S)=\sum_{s\in S}\frac{1}{N}\frac{1}{\binom{N-1}{B-1}}=\frac{B}{N\binom{N-1}{B-1}}=\frac{1}{\binom{N}{B}}\mathchar 59\relax

which proves uniformity. The probability of excluding a fixed
k
is therefore

\displaystyle\mathbf{Prob}(k\notin\tilde{\Omega})=\frac{\binom{N-1}{B}}{\binom{N}{B}}=\frac{N-B}{N}.

For any
S\subset[N]\setminus\{k\}
with
|S|=B
, Bayes’ rule gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{Prob}(\tilde{\Omega}=S\mid k\notin\tilde{\Omega})=\frac{\binom{N}{B}^{-1}}{(N-B)/N}=\frac{1}{\binom{N-1}{B}}\mathchar 59\relax

so the conditional law is uniform over the
B
-subsets of
[N]\setminus\{k\}
.
∎

Lemma 5

(Young-type bounds)
.

For any
u\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\alpha>0
,

\displaystyle-2\left\langle u\mathchar 59\relax v\right\rangle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}\left\|u\right\|^{2}+\alpha\left\|v\right\|^{2}.

(16)

Consequently, for any
\alpha>0
,

\displaystyle\left\|u+v\right\|^{2}\leq(1+\alpha)\left\|u\right\|^{2}+\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)\left\|v\right\|^{2}.

(17)

In particular,
\left\|u+v\right\|^{2}\leq 2\left\|u\right\|^{2}+2\left\|v\right\|^{2}
.

Proof.

The first bound follows from

0\leq\left\|\alpha^{-1/2}u+\alpha^{1/2}v\right\|^{2}
.
The second follows by expanding
\left\|u+v\right\|^{2}
and applying

2\left\langle u\mathchar 59\relax v\right\rangle\leq\alpha\left\|u\right\|^{2}+\alpha^{-1}\left\|v\right\|^{2}
.
∎

Lemma 6

(
[
25
,
13
,
33
,
34
]
)
.

Let
a\geq 0
and
b>0
. If

\displaystyle 0\leq\gamma\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}+b}\mathchar 59\relax

then

\displaystyle a\gamma^{2}+b\gamma\leq 1.

Moreover, if
a>0
, the bound is tight up to a factor of
2
since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}+b}\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\frac{1}{b}\right\}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{a}+b}.

Appendix C
SILAGE
, case
m\geq n

C.1
Proofs for general nonconvex functions

We first prove the one-step estimator recursions needed for the nonconvex analysis.
This is the main helper lemma for the case
m\geq n
: it tracks both the average table error and the error of the aggregated estimator used in the descent step.

Lemma 7
.

Consider Algorithm
1
(case
m\geq n
). Let
\mathcal{F}^{t}
be the filtration generated by the algorithm up to iteration
t
. Under the assumption that the internal similarity condition (
3
) holds with parameter
\delta_{2}
, the following bounds hold for the gradient estimators:

1.
Component-wise Recursion:
For any
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t})+\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

2.
Average Component Error:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

3.
Global Estimator Error:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{2p}{n}\right)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{p}{n^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-p}{n^{2}}\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{t}:=\sigma\left(x^{0}\mathchar 59\relax\{g_{i}^{0}\}_{i=1}^{n}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax x^{t}\mathchar 59\relax\{g_{i}^{t}\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the iterates and the individual gradient estimators (table entries) up to iteration
t
; in particular, the averaged estimator
g^{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}^{t}
is
\mathcal{F}^{t}
-measurable. The transition from
x^{t}
to
x^{t+1}
is deterministic given
\mathcal{F}^{t}
(since
x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma g^{t}
). However, the construction of
g^{t+1}
depends on the independent random variables realized at step
t
: the coin flip
\theta^{t}\in\{0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\}
, the index
i^{t}\sim\mathcal{U}([n])
(if
\theta^{t}=1
), and the minibatch indices
j_{i}^{t}\sim\mathcal{U}([m])
.

Fix an index
i\in[n]
. To analyze the error
\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]
, we apply the
law of total expectation
by conditioning on the update rule applied to index
i
. From Algorithm
1
, there are two mutually exclusive cases for updating
g_{i}^{t+1}
:

1.

Exact Reset (Case 1):
This occurs if
\theta^{t}=1
and
i^{t}=i
. The probability of this event is:

\mathbf{Prob}(\theta^{t}=1)\cdot\mathbf{Prob}(i^{t}=i\mid\theta^{t}=1)=p\cdot\frac{1}{n}.

In this case,
g_{i}^{t+1}:=\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})
, so the error is zero.

2.

Recursive Update (Case 2):
This occurs if
\theta^{t}=0
, or if
\theta^{t}=1
but
i^{t}\neq i
. The probability of this event is the complement of Case 1:

1-\frac{p}{n}.

In this case,
g_{i}^{t+1}:=g_{i}^{t}+\nabla f_{i\mathchar 59\relax j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathchar 59\relax j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t})
.

Applying the law of total expectation over the random choices at iteration
t
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{n}\cdot 0+\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g_{i}^{t}+\nabla f_{i\mathchar 59\relax j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathchar 59\relax j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})+\underbrace{\nabla f_{i\mathchar 59\relax j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathchar 59\relax j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t})-(\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t}))}_{:=\,\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t})+\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

The first equality separates the case where
g_{i}
is reset exactly (with probability
p/n
) from the recursive update case. The final equality relies on the standard variance decomposition
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|X+Y\right\|^{2}\right]=\left\|X\right\|^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|Y\right\|^{2}\right]
, valid here because the random vector
\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}
has zero mean conditioned on
\mathcal{F}^{t}
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]=\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t})+\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

Similarly, for the global gradient estimator
g^{t+1}
, we condition on the coin flip
\theta^{t}
. When
\theta^{t}=0
, all indices update recursively using independent minibatches
j_{i}^{t}
. Exploiting this independence, the variance of the sum becomes the sum of the variances:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=(1-p)\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\left(\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t})-(\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t}))\right)}_{\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+p\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right]

\displaystyle=(1-p)\left(\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+p\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t}-\frac{1}{n}g_{i^{t}}^{t}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]\backslash\{i^{t}\}}\left(\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t})\right)\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right]

\displaystyle=(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1-p}{n^{2}m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t})+\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+p\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t}-\frac{1}{n}g_{i^{t}}^{t}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]\backslash\{i^{t}\}}\left(\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t})\right)\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right].

The second equality applies the identity
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|X+\sum Y_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]=\left\|X\right\|^{2}+\sum{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|Y_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]
for independent zero-mean variables
Y_{i}
. The final equality expands the expectation of the stochastic noise terms
\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}
and substitutes the specific update rule for the
\theta^{t}=1
case.

To analyze the expectation conditioned on
\theta^{t}=1
, we first simplify the term inside the norm. By adding and subtracting
\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})
within the summation to recover the noise terms
\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}
, and applying the identity
\sum_{i\neq k}a_{i}=\sum_{\text{all}}a_{i}-a_{k}
, we derive the following algebraic relation:

\displaystyle g^{t}-\frac{1}{n}g_{i^{t}}^{t}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{i^{t}\}}\left(\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j_{i}^{t}}(x^{t})\right)

\displaystyle=g^{t}-\frac{1}{n}g_{i^{t}}^{t}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq i^{t}}\left(\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}+\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right)

\displaystyle=g^{t}-\frac{1}{n}g_{i^{t}}^{t}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq i^{t}}\left(\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})+\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}\right)-\frac{n-1}{n}\nabla f(x^{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq i^{t}}\left(g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq i^{t}}\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}(g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t}))-(g_{i^{t}}^{t}-\nabla f_{i^{t}}(x^{t}))\right]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq i^{t}}\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right)-\frac{1}{n}\left(g_{i^{t}}^{t}-\nabla f_{i^{t}}(x^{t})\right)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq i^{t}}\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-1}{n}\left(g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right)+\frac{1}{n}\left(g^{t}-g_{i^{t}}^{t}+\nabla f_{i^{t}}(x^{t})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq i^{t}}\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}.

Substituting this decomposition back into the main inequality, and expanding the square, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{1-p}{n}\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+p\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|\frac{n-1}{n}\left(g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right)+\frac{1}{n}\left(g^{t}-g_{i^{t}}^{t}+\nabla f_{i^{t}}(x^{t})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq i^{t}}\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right]

\displaystyle=(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{1-p}{n}\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}+p\left(\frac{n-1}{n}\right)^{2}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{p}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t}-g_{i^{t}}^{t}+\nabla f_{i^{t}}(x^{t})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{p}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\sum_{i\in[n]\backslash\{i^{t}\}}\left\|\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right].

where the last inequality follows from the fact that the
j_{i}^{t}
are independent of each other, so that the variance of the sum is the sum of the variances.

To resolve the sum over the random set
[n]\setminus\{i^{t}\}
, we rewrite the expectation using indicator variables
\mathbb{I}(i\neq i^{t})
. Since
i^{t}
is uniform on
[n]
,
\mathbf{Prob}(i\neq i^{t})=\frac{n-1}{n}
. Furthermore, the randomness of
j_{i}^{t}
is independent of
i^{t}
. Thus:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\sum_{i\in[n]\backslash\{i^{t}\}}\left\|\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}(i\neq i^{t})\left\|\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{Prob}(i\neq i^{t})\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|\xi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{t}\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{n-1}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t})+\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x^{t})+\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(n-1)\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

Note that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t}-g_{i^{t}}^{t}+\nabla f_{i^{t}}(x^{t})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|(g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t}))-(g_{i^{t}}^{t}-\nabla f_{i^{t}}(x^{t}))\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\theta^{t}=1\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{p(n-1)^{2}}{n^{2}}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{p}{n^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\frac{p}{n^{2}}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1-p}{n}\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}+\frac{p(n-1)}{n^{2}}\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{2p}{n}\right)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{p}{n^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-p}{n^{2}}\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
1

We next restate the main nonconvex convergence result for Algorithm
1
.
The proof combines the descent inequality for smooth functions with Lemma
7
to build a Lyapunov recursion.

See
1

Proof of Theorem
1
.

We rely on the variance bounds established in Lemma
7
. Specifically, item 3 of the Lemma provides the bound for the global estimator error:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{2p}{n}\right)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{p}{n^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-p}{n^{2}}\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

Additionally, item 2 of the Lemma provides the bound for the average component error:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{p}{n}\right)\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

We have the descent lemma

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq f(x^{t})+\frac{\gamma}{2}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{L}{2}-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

We assume
p>0
. We define the Lyapunov function

\Psi^{t}:=f(x^{t})-f_{\mathrm{inf}}+\frac{\gamma n}{4p}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{\gamma}{4np}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

Let us denote the Lyapunov function coefficients as
C_{1}:=\frac{\gamma n}{4p}
and
C_{2}:=\frac{\gamma}{4np}
. Taking the conditional expectation of
\Psi^{t+1}
and substituting the descent lemma, the global gradient recursion, and the component-wise gradient recursion, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\Psi^{t+1}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.f(x^{t+1})\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]-f_{\mathrm{inf}}+C_{1}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]+C_{2}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\,\right|\mathcal{F}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{f(x^{t})-f_{\mathrm{inf}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\left(\frac{L}{2}-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}}_{\text{Descent Lemma}}
