(30\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}0.5)

Table 6:
Generation parameters for the synthetic grouped logistic regression datasets.

E.2.1
Interpretation of the controlled regimes

We now explain why the construction above produces the intended relative magnitudes of
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
. The discussion relies on the Hessian interpretation of the gradient-difference similarity assumptions (formalized in Lemma
11
below). For the logistic-regression components used here, let

\displaystyle H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\mathchar 59\relax\qquad H_{i}(x):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\mathchar 59\relax\qquad H(x):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}(x).

Writing

\displaystyle\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)

\displaystyle:=\sigma(-y_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}x)\bigl(1-\sigma(-y_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}x)\bigr)\mathchar 59\relax

with
\sigma(t)=(1+\exp(-t))^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}+\lambda\nabla^{2}r(x).

Since
\lambda\nabla^{2}r(x)
is identical across components, it cancels in both
H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-H_{i}(x)
and
H_{i}(x)-H(x)
; hence the empirical similarities
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
are controlled exclusively by the geometry of the rank-one logistic terms
\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}
. The regularizer does, however, contribute to the smoothness constants. Indeed,

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}r(x)

\displaystyle=\Diag\!\left(\frac{2(1-3x_{\ell}^{2})}{(1+x_{\ell}^{2})^{3}}\right)_{\ell=1}^{d}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\left\|\nabla^{2}r(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq 2\mathchar 59\relax

so the choice
\lambda=200
adds a smoothness contribution of order
400
while leaving the nested similarity estimates untouched.

To connect this finite-sample construction to the similarity assumptions, fix
h=x-y
. By the fundamental theorem of calculus,

\displaystyle\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)+\nabla f(y)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(H_{i}(y+\tau h)-H(y+\tau h)\bigr)h\,d\tau\mathchar 59\relax

and similarly

\displaystyle\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)+\nabla f_{i}(y)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y+\tau h)-H_{i}(y+\tau h)\bigr)h\,d\tau.

Therefore, small average deviations of the group Hessians
H_{i}
from the global Hessian
H
produce a small
\delta_{1}
, while small average deviations of the component Hessians
H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
from the group Hessian
H_{i}
produce a small
\delta_{2}
.

At the population level, this mechanism is transparent. Let
M_{k}(x)
denote the average logistic Hessian contribution of latent component
k
at point
x
(excluding the common regularizer). Conditional on the group mixture
\pi_{i}
, the population analogue of the group Hessian has the form

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{i}(x)

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k}M_{k}(x)+\lambda\nabla^{2}r(x).

Writing
\overline{\pi}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i}
, the between-group deviation is governed by

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{i}(x)-\overline{H}(x)

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\pi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k}-\overline{\pi}_{k})M_{k}(x)\mathchar 59\relax

whereas the within-group dispersion is governed by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k}\left\|M_{k}(x)-\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\pi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ell}M_{\ell}(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}^{2}.

Hence
\delta_{1}
is small when the group mixtures
\pi_{i}
are similar across
i
and large when different groups place mass on different latent families. Analogously,
\delta_{2}
is small when each
\pi_{i}
concentrates on one local component (or on nearby components) and large when each
\pi_{i}
spreads mass over well-separated components.

The Dirichlet concentration parameter
\alpha_{\mathrm{dir}}
controls how closely each
\pi_{i}
tracks its prototype
\beta_{i}
. Since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\pi_{i}]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\beta}_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}(\pi_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k})

\displaystyle=\frac{\widetilde{\beta}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k}(1-\widetilde{\beta}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k})}{\alpha_{\mathrm{dir}}+1}\mathchar 59\relax

large values of
\alpha_{\mathrm{dir}}
force the realized mixtures
\pi_{i}
to concentrate around the prescribed prototypes. The small smoothing parameter
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{dir}}
only prevents degenerate zero entries in the Dirichlet parameters and has negligible effect on the intended geometry.

We now interpret each of the four regimes in turn.

Small
\delta_{1}
, Small
\delta_{2}
.

All groups share the same tightly concentrated prototype

\displaystyle\beta_{i}=0.95e_{1}+0.05e_{2}.

Up to small Dirichlet fluctuations, every group then has the same mixture; hence
H_{i}(x)
is close to
H(x)
for every
i
, and
\delta_{1}
is small. The prototype is itself nearly pure, and the geometry parameters use very small observation noise and within-family radius,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm obs}\in\{0.01\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}0.005\}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad r_{\rm intra}\in\{0.02\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}0.01\}.

Consequently, samples within each group concentrate around almost the same latent component, so the matrices
H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)
stay close to their local average
H_{i}(x)
and
\delta_{2}
is small. The large values of
\alpha_{\rm dir}
and the very small
\varepsilon_{\rm dir}=10^{-4}
further suppress accidental mixture variability.

Small
\delta_{1}
, Large
\delta_{2}
.

All groups again share the same prototype, but the prototype is now broad,

\displaystyle\beta_{i}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{r=1}^{T}e_{a_{r}}.

Because the same mixture is used for every group, the group Hessians
H_{i}(x)
remain close to each other and to
H(x)
, so
\delta_{1}
stays small. Each group, however, now mixes one active component from every macro-family. The macro-family centers are separated at scale

\displaystyle r_{\rm inter}=25\quad\text{for }m\geq n\mathchar 59\relax\qquad r_{\rm inter}=30\quad\text{for }n>m\mathchar 59\relax

and the directions defining the macro-centers are orthonormal. For components belonging to different macro-families, the corresponding rank-one matrices are therefore substantially different: at the level of the idealized centers, if
r\neq r^{\prime}
, the matrices
c_{r}c_{r}^{\top}
and
c_{r^{\prime}}c_{r^{\prime}}^{\top}
have operator-norm separation of order
r_{\rm inter}^{2}
. As a result, within a single group, the component Hessians
H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)
fluctuate strongly around
H_{i}(x)
, producing a large
\delta_{2}
. The concentration values
\alpha_{\rm dir}\in\{1000\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1500\}
keep this broad mixture stable across groups, preventing
\delta_{1}
from becoming comparable to
\delta_{2}
.

Large
\delta_{1}
, Small
\delta_{2}
.

The group prototype now depends on the assigned macro-family
s(i)
:

\displaystyle\beta_{i}=0.95e_{a_{s(i)}}+0.05e_{a_{s(i)}+1}.

Different groups are thus anchored to different macro-families. Since the macro-centers are separated by
r_{\rm inter}\in\{25\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}30\}
, the corresponding group-level Hessian averages are far apart, and the terms
H_{i}(x)-H(x)
become large on average; this is the mechanism producing a large
\delta_{1}
.

At the same time, each group is nearly pure within its assigned family, with very small within-family radius and observation noise,

\displaystyle r_{\rm intra}\in\{0.02\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}0.01\}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\sigma_{\rm obs}\in\{0.01\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}0.005\}.

Therefore, although different groups are far from one another, samples inside a fixed group concentrate around a single local component. Hence
H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)
stays close to
H_{i}(x)
within each group and
\delta_{2}
remains small. This regime cleanly isolates between-group heterogeneity from within-group heterogeneity.

Large
\delta_{1}
, Large
\delta_{2}
.

The last regime combines the two mechanisms above. We enlarge the latent dictionary to
K=64
and the number of macro-families to
T=8
, giving more distinct family identities. For each family we define

\displaystyle\gamma_{r}=\frac{1}{2}e_{a_{r}}+\frac{1}{2}e_{a_{r}+4}\mathchar 59\relax

so that
\gamma_{r}
already mixes two distinct components inside macro-family
r
. The group prototypes are

\displaystyle\beta^{(r)}

\displaystyle=0.7\gamma_{r}+0.3\gamma_{r+1}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\gamma_{T+1}=\gamma_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\beta_{i}

\displaystyle=\beta^{(s(i))}.

The dominant term
0.7\gamma_{s(i)}
gives each group a distinct macro-family anchor: since
s(i)
varies across groups, the group Hessians
H_{i}(x)
have different dominant macro-family contributions, which drives
\delta_{1}
large. The secondary term
0.3\gamma_{s(i)+1}
guarantees that every group also mixes an additional separated macro-family. Together with
r_{\rm inter}=30
and
r_{\rm intra}=0.5
, this yields substantial variation among the component Hessians inside each group, keeping
\delta_{2}
large as well.

This regime is deliberately more structured than a symmetric broad mixture. A fully symmetric broad mixture would make every group internally diverse but too similar to every other group, yielding large
\delta_{2}
but only moderate
\delta_{1}
. The cyclic family-anchored construction avoids this by assigning each group a different dominant family while retaining internal diversity through the secondary family and the two-component mixtures
\gamma_{r}
.

E.3
Estimating
L
,
\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{2}
in theory

We first record an explicit upper bound on the Lipschitz smoothness constant of the logistic regression objective (
1
) obtained directly from the component Hessian in (
69
). Because the regularizer is nonconvex, the relevant quantity for smoothness is the operator norm of the Hessian, not only a Loewner upper bound. We therefore bound
\left\|\nabla^{2}f(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}
term by term. The logistic part of the component Hessian is positive semidefinite, and since
\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\leq\tfrac{1}{4}
for all
x
,
i
, and
j
, averaging over the
nm
samples and using monotonicity of the operator norm on PSD matrices gives

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\,a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\;\leq\;\left\|\frac{1}{4nm}\,\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\;=\;\lambda_{\max}\!\left(\frac{1}{4nm}\,\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{nm\times d}
is the design matrix stacking all feature vectors
a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}
as rows. The regularizer Hessian in (
67
) is indefinite but bounded in operator norm: its diagonal entries
2(1-3x_{\ell}^{2})/(1+x_{\ell}^{2})^{3}
lie in
[-1/2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\,2]
, so
\left\|\nabla^{2}r(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq 2
. Combining the two contributions via the triangle inequality for the operator norm yields

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}f(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\;\leq\;\lambda_{\max}\!\left(\frac{1}{4nm}\,\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\right)+2\lambda\mathchar 59\relax

and a valid theoretical choice for the smoothness constant is therefore

\displaystyle L\;=\;\lambda_{\max}\!\left(\frac{1}{4nm}\,\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\right)+2\lambda.

For the similarity constants
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
, a closed-form expression of this kind is not available. Instead, we provide an equivalent characterization of the gradient-difference conditions (
71
)–(
72
) as operator-norm constraints on stacked Hessian deviations. This characterization both clarifies how the data-generation mechanisms of Section
E.2.1
translate into the magnitudes of
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
, and motivates the empirical estimation protocol of Section
E.4
.

E.3.1
Hessian characterization of
\delta_{1}
- and
\delta_{2}
-similarity

Recall the gradient-difference similarity conditions: there exist
\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2}\geq 0
such that for all
x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)+\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{1}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

(71)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)+\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}.

(72)

Because these conditions are stated in terms of gradient differences, it is useful to connect them to second-order variability when the component losses are twice continuously differentiable. The following lemma provides an equivalent characterization in terms of Hessian deviations, which directly guides how we estimate
(\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2})
in practice.

Lemma 11

(Hessian characterization of
\delta_{1}
- and
\delta_{2}
-similarity)
.

Let
f(x):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)
and

f_{i}(x):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)
.

(i)
\delta_{1}
-similarity.

Assume that
f_{i}
is twice continuously differentiable for all
i\in[n]
.
For each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define the linear operator

\mathcal{A}_{1}(x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}(x)s:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big((\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)-\nabla^{2}f(x))s\big)_{i=1}^{n}.

(73)

Then (
71
) holds if and only if

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{A}_{1}(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq\delta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(74)

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)-\nabla^{2}f(x)\right)^{\top}\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)-\nabla^{2}f(x)\right)\right)\leq\delta_{1}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(75)

(ii)
\delta_{2}
-similarity.

Assume that
f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
is twice continuously differentiable for all

i\in[n]
and
j\in[m]
.
For each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define the linear operator

\mathcal{A}_{2}(x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{nm}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}(x)s:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{nm}}\big((\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x))s\big)_{i\in[n]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\,j\in[m]}.

(76)

Then (
72
) holds if and only if

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq\delta_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(77)

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)\right)^{\top}\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)\right)\right)\leq\delta_{2}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(78)

Proof.

We prove the two equivalences separately.

(i)
\delta_{1}
-similarity.

Define the centered gradients

\displaystyle G_{i}(x):=\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x).

Then (
71
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|G_{i}(x)-G_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{1}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\forall x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(79)

(
\Rightarrow
)

Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\alpha>0
, and set

y=x+\alpha s
. Applying (
79
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\frac{G_{i}(x+\alpha s)-G_{i}(x)}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{1}^{2}\left\|s\right\|^{2}.

(80)

Since each
f_{i}
is twice continuously differentiable,

\displaystyle\frac{G_{i}(x+\alpha s)-G_{i}(x)}{\alpha}\xrightarrow[\alpha\downarrow 0]{}\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)-\nabla^{2}f(x)\right)s.

Taking
\alpha\downarrow 0
in (
80
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)-\nabla^{2}f(x)\right)s\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{1}^{2}\left\|s\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\forall x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(81)

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{1}(x)
, this is exactly

\left\|\mathcal{A}_{1}(x)s\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{1}^{2}\left\|s\right\|^{2}
for all
s
,
which is equivalent to
\left\|\mathcal{A}_{1}(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq\delta_{1}
.

(
\Leftarrow
)

Assume (
74
). Fix
x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and set
s=y-x
. By the fundamental theorem of calculus,

\displaystyle G_{i}(y)-G_{i}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x+\tau s)-\nabla^{2}f(x+\tau s)\right)s\,d\tau.

Stacking the vectors and using Jensen’s inequality in the product space

(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|G_{i}(y)-G_{i}(x)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{A}_{1}(x+\tau s)s\,d\tau\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\left\|\mathcal{A}_{1}(x+\tau s)s\right\|^{2}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\delta_{1}^{2}\left\|s\right\|^{2}\,d\tau=\delta_{1}^{2}\left\|y-x\right\|^{2}.

This is (
71
).

(ii)
\delta_{2}
-similarity.

Define the within-group centered gradients

\displaystyle G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x):=\nabla f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x).

Then (
72
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\forall x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(82)

(
\Rightarrow
)

Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\alpha>0
, and set

y=x+\alpha s
. Applying (
82
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\frac{G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x+\alpha s)-G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|s\right\|^{2}.

(83)

Since the functions
f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
are twice continuously differentiable,

\displaystyle\frac{G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x+\alpha s)-G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)}{\alpha}\xrightarrow[\alpha\downarrow 0]{}\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)\right)s.

Taking
\alpha\downarrow 0
in (
83
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)\right)s\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|s\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\forall x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(84)

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{2}(x)
, this is exactly

\left\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(x)s\right\|^{2}\leq\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|s\right\|^{2}
for all
s
,
which is equivalent to
\left\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq\delta_{2}
.

(
\Leftarrow
)

Assume (
77
). Fix
x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and set
s=y-x
. By the fundamental theorem of calculus,

\displaystyle G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)-G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x+\tau s)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x+\tau s)\right)s\,d\tau.

Stacking the vectors and using Jensen’s inequality in the product space

(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{nm}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(y)-G_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{A}_{2}(x+\tau s)s\,d\tau\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\left\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(x+\tau s)s\right\|^{2}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|s\right\|^{2}\,d\tau=\delta_{2}^{2}\left\|y-x\right\|^{2}.

This is (
72
).
∎

Practical takeaway.

Lemma
11
reduces the task of understanding
(\delta_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\delta_{2})
to quantifying
Hessian variability
across groups (for
\delta_{1}
) and within groups (for
\delta_{2}
). This perspective guides both the partition design described above and the empirical measurement protocol introduced next.

E.4
Estimating
L
,
\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{2}
in practice

We estimate the smoothness and similarity constants directly on the logistic-regression instances used in the experiments. For a data point
(a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j})
, the logistic part of the component Hessian has the form

\displaystyle H^{\rm log}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\,a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)=\sigma(-y_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}x)\bigl(1-\sigma(-y_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}x)\bigr)\mathchar 59\relax

(85)

so that
0\leq\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\leq 1/4
. The nonconvex regularizer contributes the same diagonal Hessian
\lambda\nabla^{2}r(x)
to every component, where

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}r(x)=\Diag\!\left(\frac{2(1-3x_{\ell}^{2})}{(1+x_{\ell}^{2})^{3}}\right)_{\ell=1}^{d}.

(86)

This regularization term therefore contributes to the smoothness constants but cancels exactly in the Hessian differences that define
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
.

We report two complementary families of estimates:
data-only worst-case surrogates
, obtained by replacing every
\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)
with its uniform upper bound
1/4
, and
empirical constants
, computed on a finite probe set drawn from the optimization trajectory. The empirical constants are the ones actually used for hyperparameter tuning in the main experiments.

Data-only worst-case surrogates.

These quantities are independent of the optimization trajectory: they use only
\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\leq 1/4
and
\left\|\nabla^{2}r(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq 2
. Specifically,

\displaystyle L_{\max}^{\rm wc}

\displaystyle:=\max_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\left(\tfrac{1}{4}\left\|a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\right\|^{2}+2\lambda\right)\mathchar 59\relax

(87)

\displaystyle L^{\rm wc}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{4}\,\left\|\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}+2\lambda\mathchar 59\relax

(88)

and the data-only surrogate similarity constants are

\displaystyle\delta_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}{\rm wc}}

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\frac{1}{4m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}-\frac{1}{4nm}\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{m}a_{r\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s}a_{r\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s}^{\top}\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}^{2}}\mathchar 59\relax

(89)

\displaystyle\delta_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}{\rm wc}}

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\frac{1}{4}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}-\frac{1}{4m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k}^{\top}\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}^{2}}.

(90)

These quantities are independent of the optimization trajectory and provide a useful scale for the geometry of the design matrices. Because they replace the logistic weights
\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)
by
1/4

before
centering, they should be interpreted as conservative diagnostics rather than as exact global upper bounds on the best similarity constants
\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{2}
.

Empirical constants on the optimization region.

The constants used to tune the main experiments are empirical estimates computed on a finite probe set. Starting from
x^{0}=0
, we run
20
full-gradient steps,

\displaystyle x^{s+1}=x^{s}-\eta_{\rm probe}\nabla f(x^{s})\mathchar 59\relax\qquad s=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}19\mathchar 59\relax

(91)

and collect

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\rm probe}=\{x^{0}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}x^{s_{1}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}x^{s_{5}}\}\mathchar 59\relax

(92)

where
s_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s_{5}
are five evenly spaced indices along this short trajectory. The probe stepsize is set from the precomputed worst-case smoothness estimate as

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm probe}=\frac{1}{2L^{\rm wc}}.

(93)

The empirical constants are then defined as the maxima of the relevant quantities over
\mathcal{X}_{\rm probe}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{L}

\displaystyle:=\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\rm probe}}\left\|\nabla^{2}f(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\mathchar 59\relax

(94)

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\max}

\displaystyle:=\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\rm probe}}\max_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\mathchar 59\relax

(95)

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\rm probe}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)-\nabla^{2}f(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}^{2}}\mathchar 59\relax

(96)

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\rm probe}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}^{2}}.

(97)

The empirical quantities
\widehat{\delta}_{1}
and
\widehat{\delta}_{2}
reported below are conservative operator-norm diagnostics: they average individual operator norms and therefore upper-bound the corresponding stacked-operator norms characterized in Lemma
11
. We use them as practical tuning proxies, not as exact evaluations of the best similarity constants.

For
\widehat{L}_{\max}
we exploit the rank-one form of the component Hessian, which yields the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\right\|_{\operatorname{op}}\leq\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\left\|a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\right\|^{2}+\lambda\max_{\ell}\left|\frac{2(1-3x_{\ell}^{2})}{(1+x_{\ell}^{2})^{3}}\right|.

(98)

Matrix-free operator-norm estimation.

All operator norms are computed matrix-free, through Hessian–vector products. For instance,

\displaystyle H^{\rm log}_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\,v

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\,a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}v)\mathchar 59\relax

(99)

\displaystyle H^{\rm log}_{i}(x)\,v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\,a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}v)\mathchar 59\relax

(100)

\displaystyle H^{\rm log}(x)\,v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(x)\,a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}(a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\top}v).

(101)

For smoothness estimates of
L
and
L_{\max}
, the Hessian–vector product includes the diagonal regularizer contribution
\lambda\nabla^{2}r(x)\,v
. For similarity estimates, the same regularizer is shared by all components and cancels in the centered Hessian differences, so the corresponding Hessian-difference products use only the logistic-loss part, e.g.
(H_{i}^{\rm log}(x)-H^{\rm log}(x))\,v
and
(H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\rm log}(x)-H_{i}^{\rm log}(x))\,v
.

Each operator norm was estimated by power iteration. We used
20
power iterations for both the smoothness and the similarity diagnostics, with tolerances
10^{-5}
for smoothness and
10^{-4}
for similarity estimates. The
\delta_{2}
diagnostic enumerates all
nm
components and estimates the norm of each
H_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}^{\rm log}(x)-H_{i}^{\rm log}(x)
separately; batching is used only for memory efficiency and does not subsample the components.

The resulting empirical constants
\widehat{L}
,
\widehat{L}_{\max}
,
\widehat{\delta}_{1}
, and
\widehat{\delta}_{2}
used for the synthetic experiments (rounded to two decimal places) are reported in Table
4
of Section
4
.

E.5
Hardware and implementation details

All experiments were implemented in Python 3.12.8 using PyTorch 2.5.1 (CUDA 11.8 backend, cuDNN 9.1.0). Execution was performed on a workstation featuring dual Intel Xeon Gold 6246 CPUs (24 physical cores, 48 logical threads) alongside an NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3090 (24 GB) and an RTX 2080 Ti (12 GB).

E.6
Hyperparameter tuning

E.6.1
Stepsizes

For all methods in the main synthetic comparison, we used the largest theoretically admissible constant stepsize. Concretely, after fixing the final batch size from the batch-tuning stage (and, for
D-ZeroSARAH
, the final pair
(s\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}b)
of client subset size and local batch size), we substituted these values into the theoretical stepsize expressions.
SILAGE

(m>n)
involves no batch-size tuning; for
SILAGE

(n>m)
we plug in the selected batch size
b_{\mathrm{grp}}
; for flattened
ZeroSARAH
and
SILVER
we plug in the selected flat minibatch size
b
; and for
D-ZeroSARAH
we plug in the selected pair
(s\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}b)
. For flattened
ZeroSARAH
and
D-ZeroSARAH
, whose finer-grid runs did not reach the target tolerance within the
40
-epoch budget, the selected batch size (or pair
(s\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}b)
) is the one chosen by the fallback rule described below, namely the configuration achieving the smallest final squared gradient norm at the end of the
40
-epoch budget. The resulting formulas are summarized in Table
7
, and the corresponding numerical values are listed in Table
8
. All numerical values are reported rounded to six significant digits.

the minibatch size, and
